The centrepiece of the scheme is reverse auction , where companies bid for government money to cut their carbon emissions . Here 's Greg Hunt explaining it on Lateline when Direct Action was first announced . <p> GREG HUNT , THEN OPPOSITION ENVIRONMENT SPOKESMAN ( Feb. 2010 ) : We would actually take an independent approach to purchasing the lowest cost abatement . A two-year review is also on the table . <p> What does it matter if it is on some list ? <p> Federal cabinet agreed to launch the diplomatic push to avoid the danger designation because of the reputational damage it would cause and the brand damage it could do to the reef tourism industry . <p> But more significantly , it is understood the government also feared a listing would give environment groups extra momentum in their campaign to stop the massive expansion of coal projects alongside the reef and prevent international banks from financing them . The government wants major infrastructure projects , including development of the Galilee Basin , to go ahead . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health ? <p> The UN first became alarmed in 2011 when a huge number of proposed coal and gas port projects emerged along the reef 's coast . The reef has been in a bad way . And in 2012 it was estimated 50 per cent of its coral cover was lost over the previous 27 years . <p> So if there is no listing , what has turned it around ? <p> Mr Hunt points to two recent commitments that have significantly helped Australia 's cause -- a $200 million boost to improving water quality by stopping runoff , and a ban of spoil dumping in the Great Barrier Reef Marine Park . Mr Hunt said he had visited 15 countries personally . And Australia 's environment ambassador , Peter Woolcott , and the environment department deputy secretary Kimberley Dripps have also been deployed . <p> A Senate estimates hearing this month heard $100,000 had been spent flying bureaucrats to lobby UNESCO representatives . The government has also brought international visitors , officials , foreign ministers and journalists to the reef for tours . <p> So is the reef in the clear ? <p> Not so fast . WWF campaigner Richard Leck says having plans to better protect the reef are one thing , but implementing them would be the hard part and governments must be held to their word . @@3311628 <p> United Nations conservation agency UNESCO has recommended the Great Barrier Reef not be placed on the World Heritage " in danger " list . <p> It is a significant reprieve for the Queensland and Federal governments , with an adverse listing being potentially disastrous for the tourism industry . <p> However , the draft decision from UNESCO has flagged concerns about the poor outlook for the reef and notes the World Heritage Committee should review any lack of progress in 2017 . <p> The 21 nations on UNESCO 's World Heritage Committee will decide whether to accept the decision at a meeting in Germany in a few weeks . <p> " It is recommended that the World Heritage Committee request that Australia submit an update on the progress of the Long Term Sustainability Plan progress for review by the World Heritage Centre and IUCN and that if the anticipated progress is not being made this should also be reviewed by the Committee at its session in 2017 , " the draft decision reads . <p> " It is essential that the Long Term Sustainability Plan delivers its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not face ascertained or potential danger to its Outstanding Universal Values . <p> " It is recommended the World Heritage Committee welcome the progress achieved by Australia towards a significant response . <p> " Measures that represent significant progress in responding to key World Heritage Committee requests include commitments toward restoring water quality ... restricting major port development in and adjoining the GBR World Heritage Area ... more greenhouse gases enter the atmosphere , the oceans become more acidic stunting the growth of coral . <p> Climate change is also predicted to increase the frequency and intensity of storms which are hugely damaging to the reef . <p> More immediate pressures include water quality , which has declined due to nutrient and sediment run-off from agricultural production . <p> This can lead to coral @@3313575 <p> Today 's 5-day weather predictions have the accuracy of 3-day predictions twenty years ago thanks to improved weather models . But predicting rainfall remains a challenge as it is often a localised event such as a thunderstorm covering just 10Km or less . Christian Jakob and Ailie Gallant describe the power and the limits of weather models . <h> Transcript <p> Robyn Williams : And so to climate and extreme weather . Did you know , by the way , that the father of weather forecasting was Captain Fitzroy , the man who sailed the Beagle around the world with Darwin ? Anyway , a big week for extreme weather . In Texas , in India , and the Scripps Institution of Oceanography now warns that typhoons are likely to intensify as much as by 14% under even moderate climate change impacts . <p> One of the prime weather and climate tracking centres here is at Monash University where Dr Ailie Gallant and Professor Christian Jakob do the physics and modelling . Dr Jakob has just published a paper in the journal Nature on rainfall , that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a weather and climate modeller , so I work on the beautiful computer codes that predict the weather from days to weeks to seasons ahead , and are also very similar to the computer codes that predict climate centuries ahead . It 's to me one of the unnoticed revolutions of science over the last 30 years , that this happened that we use computers . We take the laws of physics , put them into mathematics , turn them into computer code and put them into the biggest computer we can find to make weather predictions . And very few people know that this is what 's happening behind the weather forecasts they hear every morning on the ABC . <p> Robyn Williams : Is n't that interesting , because many people , even those in the trade , do n't understand how big the revolution has been . <p> Christian Jakob : Yes , it 's been pretty massive , and unlike many other fields these models are actually based on the laws of physics . So the weather is actually in output not in input . We need @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the weather like today , so that we can predict its future , and then we go ahead and make a 10-day forecast . And amazingly , over the last 30 years , we are now able to use this technology to make five-day predictions , as good as three-day predictions we had 20 years ago . <p> Robyn Williams : What about climate change is itself ? <p> Christian Jakob : Well , the same kind of models that we use in weather and seasonal prediction are also used for climate . What changes is that more components of the system need to be considered , so to make a few days of weather prediction we really only need to worry about the atmosphere . To make a forecast six months ahead we need to include the ocean because a lot of the predictability comes from the ocean . And then to do climate we need to start including biological systems , the chemistry of the atmosphere because that changes over time scales of centuries . So the models become bigger , more complex . <p> One of the weaknesses @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's in part because it happens on such small scales . Most of the rainfall events we experience are very short lived , and so that 's very , very difficult to predict , at all times scales . And one of the reasons for that is that these thunderstorms are very , very difficult to include in models . That 's in fact my special area of research , that 's what I 'm actually working on , trying to make that better . <p> Robyn Williams : How do you explain the fact that so many people who are sceptical of climate change argue that models are new-fangled nonsense , when they do n't actually know that maths , very simple arithmetic , is really a branch of mathematical modelling ? <p> Christian Jakob : Yes , I think in part it 's the result of the confusion , how those models are built . A lot of models we use in society are statistical models , often very clever , that are then used to make predictions . Climate models are completely different , they are in fact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and the result of this expression is the climate and the model actually simulates the climate without having known much of the data of the past . And we can learn what the models do well and what they do n't do well . <p> The other confusion arises I think from the fact that some of the things we can say about climate change we can really say with much confidence , like if you increase the CO2 content of the atmosphere the planet will warm . There 's no doubt about it . But there 's still a bit of uncertainty as to how much . That 's a result of our models being imperfect . And just because they are imperfect and ca n't do certain things does n't mean they ca n't do anything , so they are very good at predicting how the global temperature will change . But we still have difficulties , for instance , predicting how rainfall will change in a particular part of the world , in a very small region . <p> Robyn Williams : Your colleague is working on a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? <p> Ailie Gallant : Basically what I tend to work on is exactly the opposite end of what Christian does . So Christian is helping in model development and things like that , I tend to work more with model output and with observations to do exactly what he was talking about in terms of comparing observations to models to see where they do well , where they do n't do well , particularly in relation to climatic extremes , so things like heatwaves , droughts , things like that . <p> Robyn Williams : Yes , plenty to work on these days . <p> Ailie Gallant : Absolutely , there 's never a dull moment when it comes to climate extremes , that 's for sure . <p> Robyn Williams : And how are the models worked out when you compare them to what actually happens ? <p> Ailie Gallant : Well , in terms of extremes this is one of the tricky areas that Christian was talking about , and it really comes down to the fact that extremes are tricky beasts , they do n't happen very @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extreme , but on very , very small scales in space and in time . You know , a thunderstorm might be on the order of 10 kilometres wide . Our climate models at the moment only resolve things on the order of 100 kilometres . So we need to make estimations and assumptions on what happens on those smaller scales in the climate models and how that represents reality . And so it does get very , very tricky . <p> Larger-scale things like droughts , for example , which happen on longer time and space scales , supposedly would be easier to simulate , but when it comes to climate models , as soon as it includes circulation , so the circulation of air , the development of clouds and of course clouds to rain , every time you add another process in , things just get very tricky . <p> Robyn Williams : What did you make of Vanuatu ? <p> Ailie Gallant : In terms of Cyclone Pam that hit Vanuatu , that was a devastating event . Hurricanes unfortunately are a part of the tropics , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a particularly devastating one . It had a long time to develop and it turned into a Category 5 , which is the most severe type of hurricane that we can get , and unfortunately it just happened to hit the islands bang on , direct targets . So these things happen on occasion unfortunately . In terms of the connection to climate change , we really ca n't form any . Hurricanes happen , they happen every year , and it 's very difficult to distinguish any signal if there is one . <p> Robyn Williams : So you 're looking at the way the extreme events happen without necessarily looking at anything inevitably predictive . <p> Ailie Gallant : That 's correct , and when we are talking about the predictive capacity of climate change in the future in terms of extreme events as well , we 're not talking about specific events that happen on specific days , we are more getting an idea of how the statistics of these events will change , so the frequency of them in general , the intensity of them in general @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is that we still need to understand the mechanisms well , how they change with the climate , let alone how that might change in the future . These things are inherently complex . <p> Robyn Williams : By the way , do you go out into the field or do you stick next to your computer screen at Monash ? <p> Ailie Gallant : I certainly am an avid weather nerd , I do like to storm-chase on occasion . But for my research I tend to stick inside unfortunately . <p> Robyn Williams : Will the day come when you can predict these things ? <p> Ailie Gallant : Weather prediction at the moment is actually pretty good . You know , knowing exactly when and how strong a hurricane will be when it makes landfall on the Queensland coast three days in advance is actually not bad . We do a pretty good job of doing that , as was recently with Cyclone Nathan hitting the coast . Even Cyclone Pam was very well forecast a few days in advance . <p> In terms of future predictive capacity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which you want to predict . Climate models in the next 10 to 20 years I do n't think we 'll ever get to a space where we have a very , very reduced amount of uncertainty where we can say this will happen or that will happen . We might be able to say with some confidence about the probability of x or y happening , but I think that 's about where we are going to get . <p> Robyn Williams : Even with the ocean heating up as it is ? <p> Ailie Gallant : Absolutely , I mean , the ocean is only one component . Local processes , global processes , how does the ocean then interact with the atmosphere , how does it act on the micro physical level right through to the global level ? How does it change the circulation ? We are still not 100% sure of some of these mechanisms . So we need to understand how the mechanisms work first before we can then make predictions . <p> Christian Jakob : Well , I was going to chime in . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ heatwaves , droughts -- we have much higher confidence in predicting what might happen to those because we know the planet is going to warm and that warming will exacerbate any event that is already a heat event today . When it comes to events that have rainfall associated with them or tropical cyclones , the game is much harder . It 's a much more difficult question . And I think we just need to learn two things . One is we need to learn that perfect prediction might not be something we should be striving for . We can not remove the uncertainty but we can quantify it . And improvements in our predictive ability should reflect themselves in a reduction in this uncertainty , not in an elimination . I think this goal of eliminating uncertainty is the wrong goal , I do n't think that can be achieved . But reducing it ... <p> So the consequence of that is when we make decisions in society we need to make them on uncertain information based on risk based assessment or other type of techniques that mathematicians are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , as to how one makes decisions given uncertain information . <p> So I do n't think it 's such an alien concept . It 's often portrayed as an alien concept when it comes to weather and climate . We just need to embrace the fact that these predictions have inherent uncertainties and we need to make our best effort of making good decisions given the probability of events happening that arise from those . <p> Robyn Williams : As we do , if you think about it , in every other field . Even death and taxes are uncertain now . Law , money , politics ... weather and climate are much more solid . @@3315068 <h> Authors <h> Disclosure statement <p> Andrew Maynard does not work for , consult to , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organisation that would benefit from this article , and has no relevant affiliations . <p> Andrew J Hoffman does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> American universities are facing a crisis of relevance . There is , quite simply , a growing tension between their internal cultures and their role within society . <p> But the good news is that a growing number of us academics are taking this issue head on , exploring a broader range of models for what it means to be a scholar within society , and challenging old models that stand in the way of such progress . <p> For example , where 87% of scientists accept that natural selection plays a role in evolution , only 32% of the public agrees ; where 88% of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ safe to eat , only 37% of the public agrees . <p> In its most extreme example , actress Jenny McCarthy has been able to lead a movement in which parents choose not to vaccinate their children for fear of autism , despite the vehement rejection of that causal link by American medical institutions . <p> This dire state of affairs prompted National Geographic to devote a cover story to America 's " War on Science . " <p> At the same time , public universities are increasingly struggling to hold on to dwindling levels of state funding as state legislators profess a lack of appreciation of their value to society and parents struggle with their rising costs . <p> One Economist article opined that the American university system may be declining in much the same way that the Big 3 automakers did as their tuition costs rise , research displaces teaching as the primary focus of faculty , and administrative staffs grow to unprecedented proportions . <p> While one may worry about the future of the university in such circumstances , this drift from relevance has tremendous costs for society @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ though often neglected , role in the public and political debate on a range of issues : GMOs , climate change , gun control , health care , fiscal policy , nuclear power ; the list goes on . <h> A time for self-examination <p> Two years ago , we were part of a group of 10 faculty at the University of Michigan who began to address this problem , exploring our role as academics in public and political discourse . <p> We started with a survey of our fellow faculty 's attitudes toward academic engagement , followed by a series of faculty forums , and culminated in a May 2015 conference that brought together experts and participants from across the US to discuss the role of academic engagement in public and political discourse . <p> The three days reflected the concerns of faculty but also provided unexpected insights into the nature of emerging challenges and opportunities . <p> We discovered a commitment to public dialogue among the faculty , although there are also concerns that engagement outside the walls of academia does not receive strong institutional support , leaving academics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ academia , public engagement is viewed as a waste of time at best , and anti-intellectual at worst . <p> There was also a clear sense that this is something we should be doing as academics , but something we have neither the training , the resources , nor the institutional support to undertake effectively . From our beginning as doctoral students through our evaluation for tenure , academic research is our primary metric of excellence . <p> Four university presidents discuss academic engagement in public and political discourse at a recent University of Michigan meeting . From right to left : Michael Crow ( Arizona State University ) , Philip Hanlon ( Dartmouth College ) , Mark Schlissel ( University of Michigan ) and Teresa Sullivan ( University of Virginia ) . The panel was moderated by Andrew Hoffman ( far left ) . University of Michigan <p> In an opening discussion between the presidents of the University of Michigan , University of Virginia , Dartmouth College and Arizona State University , it was clear that , while there was general agreement on the importance of academic engagement , individual @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> University of Virginia President Teresa Sullivan spiced up the conversation by pointing out that many state legislatures not only fail to see the value of our research ; some do n't think we should be doing research at all . <p> In the face of such concerns , there was surprisingly little clarity on what we mean by " engagement , " who should be engaging , how they should be doing this and what the role of the institution is in the public and political spheres . <p> There was much talk about cultural and institutional barriers . The criteria and process of tenure and promotion review came up again and again as an institutionalized disincentive to engage . We simply do n't know how to value or even measure impact beyond the academic world where , so often , it is the citation counts -- the number of times a scholar 's work is cited by other scholars -- that determine success . <p> Yet there was also the recognition that academics need to jolt themselves out of well-worn ruts and think creatively about how to successfully @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ themselves form create barriers to engagement . <p> Reflecting this , Penn State Professor Richard Alley made the point that if he could change one thing about the academy , he would change how universities measure " excellence . " <h> The need for two-way communication <p> Throughout the conversations , though , one aspect of engagement with the public was repeated again and again : it must be more than one-way communication . <p> To be truly effective , academics need to both listen and speak , to profess deep topic knowledge but also express humility and patience in hearing what people need and what they want . <p> As stated by Michael Kennedy from Northwestern University , we need to begin with the question " How can we help you ? " <p> In fact , despite the urgency and confusion , Professor Jane Lubchenco -- former Administrator of the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration and now Oregon State University professor -- remarked that early-career academics are engaging whether we like it or not . <p> Indeed , we noted marked generational differences in this conversation . There is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has relevance beyond the ivory tower of the academy . They are already , for example , using forms of social media such as Twitter , Facebook and blogs in innovative ways that older academics neither use nor fully understand . <p> Many of the nearly 60 PhD students who attended the conference worry that academia may take some time to be welcoming to their desires . This led some to wonder whether it will take the next generation of scholars to drag the " old guard " into relevance . <p> But with this theme came questions about whether young academics that value engagement will end up voting with their feet , migrating to those universities that provide them with the opportunities , support and recognition they are looking for . Indeed , this could create a new way to differentiate universities based on the faculty they hire , the students they attract and the communities they serve . <h> Initiatives taking place across the country <p> We are still mulling over the takeaway from the conference and plan to produce a final summary report later this summer . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ norms of academia , young scholars at the University of Michigan have created the RELATE program ( Researchers Expanding Lay-Audience Teaching and Engagement ) . This program 's story also demonstrates the resistance to public engagement that still exists in some of the US 's top academic institutions . <p> In RELATE 's inaugural year , some students were so worried about their advisor 's disapproval that they kept their participation a secret . <p> Whether such innovations will have a lasting impact on US universities as a whole is unclear . <p> But if the success of these programs is anything to go by , there is a growing hunger for more diverse academic institutions and career paths that enable research and teaching excellence to be augmented by excellence in engaging in public and political discourse -- and finding relevance and value in communities outside the ivory tower . 
@@3315163 <h> Romero answers the question of who a pastor should side with <p> 4 CommentsFrank Brennan 31 May 2015 <p> Mass for the Beatification of Archbishop Oscar Romero <p> Yarralumla <p> 29 May 2015 <p> It 's a great joy for us the Church of Canberra to gather at the invitation of the El Salvador Australia Friendship Association to offer this mass as a Homage to the Blessed Oscar Romero and his work . We call to mind the dreadful civil war that ripped your country apart between 1979 and 1992 . We recall that Oscar Romero was chosen by the Vatican to be the new archbishop of San Salvador in 1977 because he was regarded as a safe spiritual leader who was acceptable to the politically powerful in El Salvador . It was thought that he would not challenge the status quo . Such predictions came to nought given the events of 12 March 1977 when his friend , the Jesuit Fr Rutillio Grande SJ was killed with two of his companions . <p> When Romero learnt about the murder of his priestly friend , the new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been laid and celebrated Mass . He then spent time listening to the stories of suffering local peasant farmers . Next morning , after meeting with his priests and advisers , Romero announced that he would not attend any state occasions nor meet with the president -- both traditional activities for his longtime predecessor -- until Grande 's death was investigated . No investigation was ever conducted , and Romero attended no state functions in his three short , but long , years as Archbishop . <p> The American poet Carolyn Forch ? who spent years in El Salvador listening to the horrific stories has often spoken in the US about ' A Poet 's Journey from El Salvador : Witness in the Light of Conscience ' . She was a good friend of Romero . She was with him the week before he was assassinated in March 1980 . This is how she told the story : <p> I met with Monsignor in the kitchen of the convent of the Carmelite Missionary Sisters , where he told me gently that it was time for me to go home , as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more needed in the United States , in the work of helping Americans to understand the struggle for justice . But I begged him to leave , as his was the first name on the death squads ' lists . He seemed so calm that afternoon , tapping his fingers on the Bible he carried with him . I realised I was in the presence of a saint . ' No ' , he said , ' my place is with my people , and now your place is with yours . ' <p> A week later on the night before he was murdered , Romero made a personal appeal to the military in a desperate attempt to stop the civilian killings in El Salvador : <p> I would like to appeal in a special way to the men of the army , and in particular to the troops of the National Guard , the police and the garrisons . Brothers , you belong to our own people . You kill your own brother peasants ; and in the face of an order to kill that is given by a man @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ kill ! ' should prevail . No soldier is obliged to obey an order counter to the law of God . No one has to comply with an immoral law . It is time now that you recover your conscience and obey its dictates rather than the command of sin .... Therefore , in the name of God , and in the name of this long-suffering people , whose laments rise to heaven every day more tumultuous , I beseech you , I beg you , I command you ! In the name of God : ' Cease the repression ! ' <p> Next day , 24 March 1980 , while he was celebrating mass in a small chapel , a lone gunman came and shot him dead at the altar . Romero lay at the altar as a martyr of the Church of the Second Vatican Council . He had what Pope Francis calls ' the smell of the sheep ' . He was a martyr who stood for love . <p> At the mass of beatification last Saturday attended by 300,000 people , eight deacons and priests carried Romero @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ altar in a glass case . Others decorated it with flowers and candles . Many concelebrating priests reached out to touch the case then making the sign of the cross . Cardinal Angelo Amato , head of the Vatican 's Congregation for Saints ' Causes declared , ' Blessed Romero is another brilliant star that belongs to the sanctity of the church of the Americas . And thanks be to God , there are many . ' So we remember too those many anonymous citizens who were gunned down during the dreadful civil war . In a message sent for the beatification , Pope Francis said Romero ' built the peace with the power of love , gave testimony of the faith with his life . ' <p> After mass on Pentecost Sunday in Rome , Pope Francis spoke with affection about Archbishop Romero ' who was killed , out of hatred of the faith , as he celebrated the Eucharist ' . Francis hailed Romero as ' a zealous pastor who , ( following ) in the example of Jesus , chose to be in the midst of his people @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cost of his life . ' <p> We recall that Romero 's life and death were bookended by the Jesuit murders , first of Fr Grande in 1977 and then on 16 November 1989 , of the six Jesuits , their housekeeper and her daughter at the Universidad Centroamerica ( UCA ) . The latter killings highlighted how dirty was the civil war and how complicit was the United States . I have recently returned from Boston College whose legendary president Fr Donald Monan SJ , accompanied by other Jesuit university presidents from the USA , went to El Salvador and sat through the trial of the soldiers indicted with those killings . He spent years lobbying US congressmen to withdraw support for the unaccountable military in El Salvador , observing , ' The intellectual architects of this crime have never been publicly identified ' or called to account . <p> Fr Ignacio Ellacuria SJ , the rector of UCA who was the main target of the later assassinations taught the American poet Forch ? that ' each moment of our life shapes the whole of our life , and that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we are certainly responsible for our response ' . When Ellacuria became rector of UCA he said that his country was ' an unjust and irrational reality that should be transformed ' and that the university needed to contribute to social change : ' It does this in a university manner and with a Christian inspiration . ' When Monan returned from El Salvador , he was fond of telling his American students : ' We must do all we can to ensure that freedom predominates over oppression , justice over injustice , truth over falsehood , and love over hatred . ' <p> Carlos Dada writing in this week 's issue of the New Yorker says : <p> Romero was not a theologian and never considered himself part of Liberation Theology , the most radical Catholic movement born of Vatican II . But he shared with the liberationists a vision of a Gospel meant to protect the poor . <p> Romero was fond of asking , ' Between the powerful and the wealthy , and the poor and vulnerable , who should a pastor side with ? ' He had @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . A pastor should stay with his people . ' <p> Carlos Dada says this ' was a political decision , but justified theologically . All of his writings include extensive biblical references , Church documents , and Papal quotations to support his assertions . ' <p> The US bishop Ken Untener when reflecting on the life of his fellow bishop Romero wrote : ' We are workers , not master builders , ministers , not messiahs . We are prophets of a future not our own . ' As we come to the table of the Eucharist , celebrating the one whose blood was poured out for us , the one who is the bread of life , let 's recall the sacramental irony that the newly Blessed one in his last homily delivered minutes before his death at the altar reflected on Jesus 's words in John 's Gospel : ' Unless the grain of wheat falls to the ground and dies it remains alone . But if it dies it produces much fruit . ' We Australians pray in thanks for those of you from El Salvador who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dreadful civil war , and we pray that your commitment to peace and reconciliation for all continues to be one of the great fruits of the one we celebrate this evening . Let 's join with Pope Francis who prayed this week , ' May those who have Archbishop Romero as a friend of faith , those who invoke him as protector and intercessor , those who admire his image , find in him the strength and courage to build the Kingdom of God , to commit to a more equal and dignified social order . " <p> The Doctors for the Environment Australia report , called ' No Time for Games - Children 's Health and Climate Change ' , has called on the Federal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effects of climate change . <p> " It 's not too late to mitigate and adapt and this report is a message for political leaders that we have got to up the targets on reducing emissions , leave fossil fuels in the ground and drastically increase our use of renewable energy . " <p> The report 's authors and Professor Stanley said Australia must use the Paris climate change talks to reverse its lagging efforts on reducing greenhouse gases and commit to much more ambitious emissions reductions . <p> They said the 2020 target of a 5% reduction on 2000 levels should be boosted to 30 per cent . <p> " We really need to up those targets significantly , as other countries around the world are doing , " Professor Stanley said . <p> They said the target needed to be bolstered to 30 per cent . <p> Former Australian of the Year Professor Fiona Stanley says children are the most vulnerable to the effects of climate change.Kate Rieben <p> Report author Dr Sallie Forrest said even she was shocked by what she discovered while compiling the research . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ suffer the most effects , yet they are the least responsible , " she said . <p> " And the most vulnerable are children in developing countries , who suffer 88 per cent of the burden of the impacts of climate change .
